# Claude Code / Agentic Coding Frameworks — Deep Comparison

> Research compiled from a multi-agent investigation (June 2026): the landscape was
> enumerated across multiple search angles, each framework deep-researched by a
> dedicated agent, and the risky claims (maintenance, stars, Claude Code integration,
> security/trust) independently fact-checked before synthesis. ~60 agents, ~2.7M tokens,
> 1,071 tool calls. 25 frameworks profiled.

**Caveat:** star counts and "last release" dates are point-in-time (June 2026) and move fast.

## Tiers

### Tier 1 — Best overall picks for working WITH Claude Code
**Superpowers, Claude Task Master, GitHub Spec Kit, wshobson/agents**

First-party-quality nativeness, trustworthy or org-level governance, and real capability without dealbreaker risks. Superpowers is the strongest pure methodology (Anthropic-marketplace-listed, benign single hook, disciplined TDD). Task Master is the most production-credible spec/task engine (company-backed, OIDC+provenance npm publishing, native plugin+MCP+subagents). Spec Kit is GitHub-org-governed and tool-agnostic. wshobson/agents gives huge native breadth with the lowest-magic install (just Markdown plugins).

### Tier 2 — Strong, situational, or with a notable tradeoff
**OpenSpec, BMAD-METHOD, cc-sdd, Agent OS, Ralph Wiggum, awesome-claude-code-subagents (VoltAgent), Claude Squad**

Genuinely useful and reasonably safe, but each has a real caveat: OpenSpec/cc-sdd/Agent OS are solo-led with opaque-ish governance; BMAD is powerful but heavy with high process overhead and concentrated bus-factor; Ralph Wiggum is first-party but a niche autonomy primitive with runaway-cost risk; VoltAgent's subagents are explicitly unaudited; Claude Squad is an external tmux orchestrator (no native integration) under AGPL. All are fine choices if their specific shape matches your need.

### Tier 3 — Niche / specialist tools, not general workflow frameworks
**claude-code-router, Claude Code Agent Farm, Spec-Flow, awesome-claude-code (hesreallyhim), awesome-claude-code-toolkit (rohitg00)**

These solve narrow problems or are directories rather than frameworks. claude-code-router is a model-routing proxy (different category, MITM trust surface, active typosquat malware in its category). Agent Farm is a mass-parallel sweep tool requiring --dangerously-skip-permissions. Spec-Flow is a tiny, slowing, solo project (~88 stars). The two awesome-lists are discovery bookmarks, not things you 'use with' Claude Code.

### Tier 4 — Avoid for this purpose (wrong tool or unacceptable trust profile)
**GSD Core / GSD (Get Shit Done), Claude-Flow / Ruflo, Kiro**

Kiro is NOT a Claude Code framework at all — it is a competing standalone AWS IDE, so it cannot satisfy the premise. GSD carries an active supply-chain risk (original npm publish keys held by a vanished maintainer linked to a $GSD token rug-pull) plus advisory-only security hooks. Claude-Flow/Ruflo has a documented destructive obfuscated preinstall, prompt-injection in MCP tool descriptions, an independent audit alleging ~97% of tools are non-functional stubs, and implausible self-reported benchmarks. None are appropriate default recommendations.

## Ranking

### #1 — Superpowers
- **Why:** The best-governed serious methodology for Claude Code: accepted into Anthropic's OFFICIAL plugin marketplace (a real first-party credibility signal beyond raw stars), uses native Skills + a single benign SessionStart hook (independently confirmed to make no network calls), and enforces genuinely useful discipline (hard-gated brainstorm, RED-GREEN TDD, subagent-per-task to beat context exhaustion). MIT, ~222k stars, active. It is prompts + one hook, so it is trivial to audit and uninstall.
- **Best for:** Senior devs/teams shipping non-trivial features (~3+ files) who want enforced design-before-code and TDD without a proprietary runtime.
- **Watch out:** Heavy process overhead and higher token/latency cost on small tasks; coercive 'you ABSOLUTELY MUST' prompt language can over-trigger; single-maintainer bus-factor (obra ~355 commits).

### #2 — Claude Task Master
- **Why:** Most production-credible of the spec/task engines. Company-backed (Hamster), npm published via GitHub Actions OIDC WITH provenance attestation (a rare, strong supply-chain signal), zero advisories/CVEs/malware. First-class Claude Code support three ways: native plugin (~52 slash commands + 3 subagents), MCP server, and a zero-API-key 'claude-code' provider mode. Tunable token footprint (core/standard/all). Tool-agnostic so low editor lock-in.
- **Best for:** Solo devs and small teams doing larger multi-step features who will write a PRD and want a persistent, dependency-aware task graph driving execution.
- **Watch out:** MIT + Commons Clause (cannot resell/host it); OSS funnels toward paid Hamster Studio; ~36 MCP tools is ~21k tokens of context if you load them all; permissionMode 'bypassPermissions' exists and is footgun-able.

### #3 — GitHub Spec Kit
- **Why:** The trust-and-governance pick: owned by the github org, MIT, very high release cadence, and still actively driven by its creator (localden). Tool-agnostic across 30+ agents with explicit 'no lock-in' design; integrates with Claude Code via the standard .claude/commands/ Markdown slash-command format (plus optional skills mode) — nothing proprietary. No spec-kit-specific CVE/malware found.
- **Best for:** Greenfield/net-new features and teams standardizing one auditable spec->plan->tasks workflow across multiple agents/IDEs.
- **Watch out:** Heavy waterfall-like ceremony (a Scott Logic review found iterative prompting ~10x faster on first increment); brittle to requirement pivots; weak on brownfield; frequent breaking flag changes (--ai removed in v0.10.0); no auto-generated CLAUDE.md, so Claude isn't aware of conventions by default.

### #4 — wshobson/agents
- **Why:** Best native breadth-per-effort: 192 subagents + 156 skills + 102 commands + 16 orchestrators delivered through Claude Code's OWN plugin marketplace/subagent/skills primitives — no runtime, just inspectable Markdown+JSON. Active (commits within days, Dependabot CI), MIT, ~36.5k stars, cost-aware model tiering, and a genuine plugin-eval quality framework. Multi-harness from one source.
- **Best for:** Polyglot/full-stack devs already on Claude Code who want a ready-made specialist library and will install selectively (1-3 plugins).
- **Watch out:** Documented context-window blowout (issue #93: ~404k vs 15k recommended tokens) if you install broadly; auto-delegation is unreliable so you often must name agents; solo maintainer, NO tagged releases (pin a SHA); breadth-over-depth prompt quality varies.

### #5 — OpenSpec
- **Why:** The lightweight, brownfield-first spec framework. Lower ceremony and far cheaper than BMAD, MIT, ~54k stars, active (~monthly releases), and tool-agnostic across ~30 tools. Integrates with Claude Code via generated slash commands + skills (no MCP/plugin/hooks needed). A clean propose->apply->archive lifecycle with specs/ as the single source of truth.
- **Best for:** Teams on EXISTING codebases who want disciplined, reviewable AI changes without heavyweight multi-agent personas, especially mixed-tool shops.
- **Watch out:** Solo-publisher governance (tabish_) under an org with no public members; no SECURITY.md; enforcement is model-instruction-following, not code-enforced gates; expanding CLI surface (workspaces, context-store) cuts against the 'lightweight' pitch.

### #6 — BMAD-METHOD
- **Why:** The most capable full-SDLC methodology: role-specialized agents (PM/architect/SM/dev/QA/UX), persistent PRD/architecture/story artifacts, scale-adaptive depth, native Claude Code Skills install, and genuinely multi-tool. Real legal entity (BMad Code, LLC), identifiable founder, near-daily commits, MIT (+trademark), no published advisories.
- **Best for:** Teams/serious solo devs on non-trivial greenfield projects wanting auditable, role-separated, spec-driven development with a real paper trail.
- **Watch out:** High process overhead (issue #2003 estimates ~10-15x slower on small work); steep learning curve and module sprawl; weak built-in quality gates can ship shallow fixes; concentrated bus-factor; heavy plugin-ecosystem confusion (use the official npx installer, not the bmad@ third-party identifiers).

### #7 — cc-sdd
- **Why:** A clean one-command (npx cc-sdd@latest) Kiro-style SDD harness delivered as native Claude Code Agent Skills, with a strong autonomous /kiro-impl loop (separate implementer + reviewer + fresh-context debug subagent, TDD). MIT, active, no advisories, genuinely multi-platform.
- **Best for:** Claude Code/Codex users wanting a structured discovery->requirements->design->tasks->implementation pipeline with human approval gates and persistent steering memory.
- **Watch out:** Solo maintainer (User account, not org); no SECURITY.md; 6 of 8 supported agents are Beta; rapid v2->v3 churn makes external tutorials stale; ceremony is overkill for small edits.

### #8 — Agent OS
- **Why:** A refreshingly humble, standards-first framework: codifies your conventions into version-controlled Markdown and injects them as Claude Code Skills, deferring spec authoring to Plan Mode and implementation to the model. Minimal attack surface (no server/MCP/hooks), MIT, org-named account.
- **Best for:** Teams/multi-project orgs that want to codify and auto-apply coding standards across AI agents, and to modernize legacy codebases.
- **Watch out:** Solo-dominated (~79% one author); bash-only installer with real fresh-install/Windows friction; no releases since v3.0.0 (Jan 2026); frequent breaking majors; lots of outdated docs (and a persistent false 'it's an MCP server' myth).

### #9 — Ralph Wiggum (official Anthropic plugin)
- **Why:** First-party Anthropic plugin (lowest supply-chain risk), tiny and fully auditable (one Stop hook + state file), and a genuinely useful autonomy primitive: loops a prompt until a completion promise or max-iterations, accumulating progress in files/git rather than context.
- **Best for:** Experienced devs running well-scoped, machine-verifiable tasks (TDD-to-green, mechanical refactors) where success is checkable and they'll cap iterations.
- **Watch out:** Runaway cost (~$10/hr; infinite loop by default if you omit --max-iterations); completion is a single literal string match; no semantic verifier; practical unattended use pairs it with --dangerously-skip-permissions, so sandbox it; bash/jq/perl (nix/WSL).

### #10 — awesome-claude-code-subagents (VoltAgent)
- **Why:** 154+ ready-made subagents via a native Claude Code plugin marketplace; org-backed (VoltAgent), MIT, ~21.5k stars, easy modular install/uninstall (plain Markdown), per-agent model routing. Good fast way to add specialist personas.
- **Best for:** Claude Code users who want to cherry-pick a few high-value specialist agents (code-reviewer, security auditor, language expert) without writing prompts.
- **Watch out:** Explicitly UNAUDITED (repo's own 'as is, we do not audit' disclaimer) and agents can grant Bash/write tools — review frontmatter before installing; ZERO releases/tags (track a moving main); Claude-Code-only; partly a marketing funnel for the separate VoltAgent TS framework.

### #11 — Claude Squad
- **Why:** Best external multi-agent parallelizer: a Go TUI that runs many agent CLIs at once, each isolated in its own tmux session + git worktree, with a diff-review-then-ship flow. Tool-agnostic, lightweight single binary, actively maintained.
- **Best for:** Terminal-comfortable devs/small teams who want to fan out many independent tasks in parallel (or compare Claude vs Codex vs Gemini) and review each in isolation.
- **Watch out:** NOT a native integration (it just drives the claude CLI in a pane — no plugins/skills/MCP/hooks); hard deps on tmux + gh (weak on native Windows); AGPL-3.0 (commercial blocker for some); effectively solo-maintained; parallel agents multiply token spend and merge conflicts.

### #12 — claude-code-router
- **Why:** Best-in-class for one specific job — routing Claude Code's requests to cheaper/alternative/local models (DeepSeek, Gemini, Ollama, etc.) while keeping the Claude Code UX. ~35k stars, MIT, task-aware routing. Useful for cost control, not a workflow framework.
- **Best for:** Cost-sensitive or offline-leaning power users who love Claude Code's interface but want to swap the underlying model or run without an Anthropic account.
- **Watch out:** It is a MITM proxy that sees ALL your code/prompts; its category is actively weaponized by typosquat/clone malware (FauxCode etc.) so install the EXACT @musistudio package; solo maintainer, no GitHub releases (npm-only), no release in ~5 months; non-Claude models often break Claude Code's tool-calling.

### #13 — Claude Code Agent Farm
- **Why:** Niche but real: a Python+tmux supervisor that runs 20-50 parallel Claude Code sessions with lock-based coordination to brute-force large mechanical sweeps (lint/type/test fixes) across 34 stacks. Known, non-anonymous maintainer.
- **Best for:** Power users on large mature codebases clearing thousands of mypy/ruff/tsc/eslint errors or applying a consistent best-practices pass in parallel.
- **Watch out:** Mandates --dangerously-skip-permissions for ALL agents (huge blast radius — sandbox/VM only); solo project, no tagged releases, non-standard 'MIT + OpenAI/Anthropic Rider' license; nix/tmux-only; ~500MB RAM/agent and many billed sessions; best-effort (not guaranteed) conflict avoidance.

### #14 — Spec-Flow
- **Why:** A vendored-into-your-repo SDD pipeline (spec->plan->tasks->implement->optimize->ship) using native Claude Code slash commands/subagents/skills/hooks, MIT, with quality-gate and multi-agent review phases. Functional but tiny and slowing.
- **Best for:** A solo dev already on Claude Code who wants a self-owned, editable, document-first pipeline and doesn't mind the Python/yq/Git prerequisites.
- **Watch out:** Very low adoption (~88 stars, ~415 npm downloads/mo) means little community vetting; ~99% solo maintainer; a postinstall script runs on npm install; release cadence already slowed (4-month gap); advertised 'auto-compaction/token budgets' are unbenchmarked.

### #15 — awesome-claude-code (hesreallyhim)
- **Why:** The canonical hand-curated DISCOVERY directory (~46k stars, editorially vetted, MIT-adjacent). Excellent as a starting bookmark to find skills/subagents/hooks/commands — but it is not something you install or 'use with' Claude Code.
- **Best for:** Anyone evaluating the Claude Code ecosystem who wants a judgment-curated index to discover individual tools to adopt.
- **Watch out:** Zero runtime/capability — it's a list; CC BY-NC-ND 4.0 restricts commercial reuse; its README ToC is currently mid-migration ('I. TODO'); inclusion is an endorsement signal, NOT a security audit (vet each linked third-party repo yourself); name-collision with several look-alike marketplaces.

### #16 — awesome-claude-code-toolkit (rohitg00)
- **Why:** A broad catalog + plugin marketplace (135 agents/35 skills/176+ plugins) with native /plugin marketplace support, monthly tagged releases, and a credible public maintainer. Useful for discovery and cherry-picking.
- **Best for:** Tinkerers/educators who want one marketplace to browse and pull one or two specific agents/hooks/MCP configs into their setup.
- **Watch out:** Mostly POINTERS to third-party repos of wildly varying quality/security; inflated marketing metrics (400k SkillKit skills, borrowed star counts); curl|bash install vector and lifecycle hooks that run Bash/Python; solo personal-account governance; large unreviewed PR backlog.

### #17 — GSD Core / GSD (Get Shit Done)
- **Why:** Technically excellent design (context-rot mitigation via fresh-context subagents, durable on-disk STATE/CONTEXT, native CC plugin + 7 lifecycle hooks, ~100 commands/36 agents, published security audits) — but ranked low because of its trust profile, not its engineering.
- **Best for:** Only for users who deliberately want a CC-native context-engineering workflow, will install ONLY @opengsd/gsd-core, pin versions, and review hooks/permissions before granting autonomy.
- **Watch out:** ACTIVE supply-chain risk: original npm publish keys remain with the vanished original maintainer (linked to a $GSD token rug-pull); security hooks are advisory-only ('warn but never block'); @~/ refs can inline host secrets; new continuity-fork governance (bus-factor ~1); heavy install/identity confusion.

### #18 — Claude-Flow / Ruflo
- **Why:** Maximalist, hype-heavy multi-agent swarm framework with a real, useful memory layer (HNSW vector + SQLite) — but the orchestration claims are largely contradicted by independent audits and it has a documented malicious-behavior history. Recommended only as sandboxed experimentation.
- **Best for:** Researchers wanting to experiment with the (genuinely working) persistent vector-memory subsystem in a disposable VM with pinned >=3.6.x versions.
- **Watch out:** Documented DESTRUCTIVE obfuscated npm preinstall (v3.1.0-alpha.55..3.5.2); prompt-injection in MCP tool descriptions (adding the owner as a repo contributor); audit alleges ~290/300 tools are non-functional stubs; implausible self-reported benchmarks (84.8% SWE-bench would beat frontier models); solo control of npm 'latest', no SECURITY.md. Do NOT use in production or with sensitive data.

### #19 — Kiro
- **Why:** Disqualified from the premise: Kiro is NOT a Claude Code framework/plugin/skill — it is a standalone, closed-source AWS IDE+CLI that COMPETES with Claude Code (both happen to run Claude). The only interop is peer-to-peer over ACP. It cannot be 'used with' Claude Code in the sense the question asks.
- **Best for:** AWS-centric teams wanting an enforced spec-driven IDE as an ALTERNATIVE to Claude Code — not as an addition to it.
- **Watch out:** Wrong category for this comparison; closed-source/AWS lock-in; documented patched IDE RCE CVEs (CVE-2026-0830, CVE-2026-10591); credit-pricing complaints; free/individual tiers collect content for training by default unless you opt out.

## Recommendations by use case

- **Solo dev, small projects / quick features:** Claude Task Master for structured multi-step features (write a PRD, let it drive the task graph), OR Ralph Wiggum for well-scoped machine-verifiable loops (always cap iterations). For tiny one-off edits, skip frameworks entirely and use stock Claude Code Plan Mode — every framework here is overhead on trivial work. If you want lightweight standards/spec discipline, OpenSpec.
- **Large / complex codebases:** Superpowers (subagent-per-task + plans-on-disk specifically targets context exhaustion on 15+ file features) as the primary methodology. Pair with wshobson/agents for on-demand domain specialists (install selectively to avoid the context blowout). For brownfield specifically, OpenSpec is the best fit. For mass mechanical cleanup (thousands of lint/type errors), Claude Code Agent Farm — but only in a sandbox.
- **Teams:** GitHub Spec Kit (github-org governance, auditable spec->plan->tasks trail, no vendor lock-in) for the safest shared baseline. BMAD-METHOD if you want richer role separation and PRD/architecture/story artifacts and accept the overhead. Agent OS if the goal is enforcing shared coding standards across the team. Avoid solo-maintained and trust-flagged options (GSD, Claude-Flow) for team-critical work.
- **Multi-tool shops (Claude Code + Cursor/Copilot/Gemini/Codex):** GitHub Spec Kit or OpenSpec (both explicitly tool-agnostic with plain-Markdown artifacts and 30+ agent support). BMAD-METHOD and cc-sdd are also genuinely cross-tool. For running multiple agent CLIs in parallel side-by-side, Claude Squad. Avoid Claude-Code-only options (VoltAgent subagents, SuperClaude, Ralph Wiggum) as your standard.
- **Beginners:** Superpowers via Anthropic's official marketplace (one-command install, first-party-vetted, teaches good habits) is the safest on-ramp. SuperClaude Framework is a gentler, zero-config opinionated baseline if you want a ready-made command/agent set. Steer clear of heavy methodologies (BMAD's ~2-month learning curve), autonomy loops (Ralph), and anything trust-flagged (GSD, Claude-Flow) until you can read hooks/permissions yourself.

## Key takeaways

- Trust beats features. The two most feature-dense entries (Claude-Flow/Ruflo and GSD) are the two with the worst supply-chain stories — a documented destructive preinstall + stub-orchestration audit, and vanished-maintainer npm keys tied to a token rug-pull respectively. Engineering ambition does not offset an active trust problem.
- First-party or org-backed governance is the single most reliable signal: Superpowers (Anthropic marketplace), Ralph Wiggum (Anthropic), GitHub Spec Kit (github org), and Task Master (company-backed with OIDC+provenance npm publishing) are the safest defaults. Most others are solo-maintained — usable, but pin versions and review hooks.
- Match the weight class to the task. Spec-driven frameworks (Spec Kit, BMAD, OpenSpec, cc-sdd) consistently cost 5-15x more time/tokens on small work; one review found iterative prompting ~10x faster than Spec Kit on a first increment. Use heavy methodology only for non-trivial, multi-file, longer-lived work.
- Kiro is a category error for this question — it's a competing standalone AWS IDE, not a Claude Code add-on. Don't pick it expecting to extend Claude Code; the only link is peer-to-peer ACP.
- Two of the most-starred 'frameworks' (hesreallyhim's and rohitg00's awesome-lists) are directories, not tools you run. They're great for discovery, but inclusion in a list is an endorsement signal, not a security audit — vet every third-party plugin/hook/MCP server you install from them, since those execute code with your permissions.
- Native integration mechanism matters: prefer tools using Claude Code's own primitives (plugins, Skills, slash commands, subagents) over external wrappers. Superpowers, wshobson/agents, Task Master, OpenSpec, cc-sdd, and Agent OS are all low-magic and auditable; Claude Squad and Agent Farm are external orchestrators with no native hooks into Claude Code.
- If you only adopt one thing: Superpowers for methodology/discipline, or Claude Task Master if you think in PRDs and task graphs. Add wshobson/agents on top for specialist breadth (installed selectively). That trio covers most solo-to-team Claude Code workflows with the best risk-adjusted payoff.

## Completeness notes

Frameworks the completeness critic flagged (researched in a remediation round where major):
- **Everything Claude Code (ECC)** (github.com/affaan-m/ECC (a.k.a. affaan-m/everything-claude-code)) — Massively popular 'agent harness' framework (reported 100K-200K+ GitHub stars across 2026, putting it at or above the Superpowers/Spec-Kit/GSD tier) bundling 60+ agents, 250+ skills, a hook system, memory/instinct persistence, security scanning, and the NanoClaw multi-agent orchestration layer across Claude Code/Codex/Cursor/OpenCode. It is a top-tier, frequently-cited Claude Code framework and is conspicuously absent from the set.
- **claude-code-templates (aitmpl / CC-Templates)** (github.com/davila7/claude-code-templates) — ~28K-star CLI + web (aitmpl.com) 'package manager' for Claude Code that installs 1000+ agents, commands, skills, hooks, settings and MCP configs, plus analytics/monitoring. One of the most widely-used configuration/distribution frameworks in the ecosystem; comparable in reach to the awesome-* toolkits already in the set but is itself a tool/framework, not just a list.

Comparison dimensions to keep in mind:
- Distribution / installation mechanism: how a framework is delivered and updated (npx/CLI installer like cc-sdd & claude-code-templates, plugin marketplace, git submodule/clone, copy-paste prompts). Strongly affects adoption friction and update path.
- Cross-harness / cross-agent portability: whether the framework is Claude-Code-only or also targets Codex, Cursor, OpenCode, Gemini CLI, Aider (ECC, ccpm, cc-sdd, Spec-Kit, BMAD all advertise multi-agent support; Superpowers/GSD are more Claude-Code-centric). A key buyer-decision axis.
- Context/token-management strategy: explicit context-engineering, fresh-context subagents, compaction, and token-budget optimization (central to GSD, ECC, Claude-Flow) vs frameworks that don't address context window pressure.
- Persistent memory / continuous learning: whether the framework persists cross-session memory or 'instincts'/learned patterns (ECC, Claude-Flow, Agent OS) vs stateless prompt-only frameworks.
- Parallelism & isolation model: single-agent loop vs multi-agent orchestration, and git-worktree isolation for safe parallel execution (ccpm, Claude-Flow, Claude Squad, Agent Farm) vs sequential single-context frameworks.
- Governance / safety / security tooling: built-in security review, permission/hook guardrails, sandboxing (ECC AgentShield, security-review skills) vs frameworks with no safety layer — increasingly a differentiator for team/enterprise use.
- Maturity & maintenance health: star count, contributor count, release cadence, and corporate vs solo/community backing (e.g., Vibe Kanban's company shutdown, Crystal->Nimbalyst rebrand) as a sustainability dimension.
- Human-in-the-loop / review surface & artifact source-of-truth: where state lives and how humans review it (GitHub Issues in ccpm, spec/ dir state machine in OpenSpec, Kanban boards in GUI orchestrators, PR-based review) — affects team collaboration fit.

Verdict: One unambiguous major miss — Everything Claude Code (ECC, affaan-m/ECC), a top-tier agent-harness framework reported at 100K-200K+ stars in 2026, squarely comparable to Superpowers/Spec-Kit/BMAD/GSD and absent from the set. I recommend adding it. Second, claude-code-templates (davila7, ~28K stars) is a borderline-major distribution/config framework worth including, though it is a component installer rather than a workflow methodology so it may already be partially represented by the awesome-* toolkit entries. Being strict per instructions, I excluded several notable-but-sub-tier or different-category tools: CCPM (automazeio/ccpm, ~8K stars) is well-known but below the 40K+ flagship tier; GUI/orchestrator apps (Conductor, Vibe Kanban [company shut down April 2026, now community Apache-2.0], Crystal->Nimbalyst, Claude Squad) are a different category (parallel-agent GUIs, not prompt/workflow frameworks) and Claude Squad is already in the set; full standalone agents/IDEs (Cursor, Codex, Aider, Cline, Roo Code, OpenCode) are not Claude-Code frameworks-on-top so out of scope. Note: web-search star figures for ECC and a few others varied widely by article date and some appeared inflated/hallucinated — I cross-checked the GitHub repo pages directly, where ECC consistently shows the largest counts. Source caveat: exact live star numbers should be re-verified on github.com at time of use."

## Full framework profiles

### Superpowers
`https://github.com/obra/superpowers`

**Category:** Agentic skills framework + opinionated software-development methodology delivered as a coding-agent plugin (primarily a Claude Code plugin, with multi-harness support)

**One-liner:** A markdown "skills" library that imposes a disciplined brainstorm -> plan -> subagent-driven-TDD-execution workflow on coding agents, installed as a Claude Code plugin (also works on Codex, Cursor, Gemini CLI, OpenCode, Copilot CLI, Factory Droid).

**Philosophy:** "Skills are what give your agents Superpowers." Agents should consult documented procedures before acting, talk through a plan before implementing, and execute in small, tested, reviewed units. Systematic over ad-hoc; design-before-code (hard-gated); true RED-GREEN TDD plus YAGNI/DRY; evidence-based verification before declaring done; and reuse via composable, shareable markdown skills.

**How it works:** Superpowers is not code that runs your project; it is a curated set of prompt/instruction files ("skills") plus a SessionStart hook that steers the agent's behavior. Confirmed mechanics from the repo (obra/superpowers @ v5.1.0):

PHILOSOPHY: The author (Jesse Vincent, of Prime Radiant) built it to systematize his own agent workflow into "composable skills" - documented procedures the agent MUST consult before acting. Core belief: agents should not jump straight to code; they should pressure-test intent first, plan explicitly, then execute in small isolated units with tests and review. The README phrases the goal as producing "an implementation plan that's clear enough for an enthusiastic junior engineer with poor taste, no judgement, no project context, and an aversion to testing to follow." He notes he used Cialdini-style persuasion language to get LLMs to actually obey the skills.

PHASES (the methodology, each backed by a skill folder under /skills): 1) brainstorming - Socratic, one-question-at-a-time design refinement with a HARD-GATE forbidding any code until the user approves a presented design (applies even to 'simple' tasks); 2) using-git-worktrees - creates an isolated branch/worktree workspace; 3) writing-plans - breaks work into ~2-5 minute tasks with exact file paths and verification steps; 4) subagent-driven-development - dispatches a fresh subagent per task with a two-stage review; 5) test-driven-development - enforces RED-GREEN-REFACTOR; 6) requesting-code-review / receiving-code-review - severity-rated review against the plan; 7) finishing-a-development-branch - merge/PR options and cleanup. Other skills: systematic-debugging, verification-before-completion, dispatching-parallel-agents, executing-plans, writing-skills, using-superpowers. (Verified: exactly 14 skill folders on main as of 2026-06-09.)

SKILLS FORMAT: each skill is a SKILL.md with YAML frontmatter (name + a 'description' that doubles as the trigger/when-to-use text, e.g. brainstorming's description starts 'You MUST use this before any creative work...'). Bodies use tags like <HARD-GATE>, <EXTREMELY-IMPORTANT>, <SUBAGENT-STOP> plus checklists and flow diagrams. Some skills carry helper scripts and companion prompt files (e.g. brainstorming/ has scripts/, spec-document-reviewer-prompt.md, visual-companion.md).

ARTIFACTS IT CREATES: design/spec docs, markdown implementation plans persisted to disk (progress lives in files, not just context), git worktrees/branches, test files, and PRs.

SUBAGENTS: real Claude Code subagents (Task tool) are dispatched one-per-task so each gets a fresh, scoped context - this is the mechanism the framework leans on to avoid context exhaustion on large features.

**Claude Code integration:** Primarily a NATIVE Claude Code plugin, distributed both via Anthropic's official marketplace and obra's own marketplace. Integration surface (verified from repo files + the live API):

- PLUGIN: .claude-plugin/plugin.json declares name 'superpowers', version 5.1.0, MIT, author Jesse Vincent <jesse@fsck.com>, description "Core skills library for Claude Code: TDD, debugging, collaboration patterns, and proven techniques". No bundled slash 'commands' dir in core - the v5.1.0 release notes explicitly state "Legacy slash commands removed" (deprecated stubs like /brainstorm, /execute-plan, /write-plan were deleted). Functionality is delivered through SKILLS, not custom slash commands. (Note: post-5.1.0 issues #756/#833 report some installs still surfacing deprecated command aliases; PR #727 cleans these up.)
- SKILLS: invoked through Claude Code's native Skill tool. Content loads on demand. 14 skill folders confirmed on main.
- HOOKS: a SessionStart hook (hooks/hooks.json, matcher 'startup|clear|compact', synchronous) runs hooks/run-hook.cmd -> hooks/session-start. The session-start bash script injects the FULL text of the using-superpowers skill into the session as context. It emits platform-specific JSON: hookSpecificOutput.additionalContext for Claude Code, additional_context (snake_case) for Cursor, top-level additionalContext for Copilot CLI/SDK. It also prints a migration WARNING if a legacy ~/.config/superpowers/skills dir exists (skills moved to ~/.claude/skills). VERIFIED BENIGN: the hook performs only local file reads and existence checks and makes no network calls.
- NO core MCP server: the core plugin ships skills+hook only. A separate, OPTIONAL 'private-journal-mcp' MCP server is offered as a distinct plugin in obra/superpowers-marketplace (semantic-search journaling), but it is not part of the core install.
- OFFICIAL MARKETPLACE: accepted into anthropics/claude-plugins-official via PR #148 (reported merged 2026-01-15), so 'superpowers@claude-plugins-official' is a real, first-party-listed install.
- MULTI-TOOL: not Claude-Code-only. The repo carries .codex-plugin, .cursor-plugin, .opencode, gemini-extension.json, AGENTS.md, GEMINI.md, and the hook branches per-harness. Supported: Claude Code, Codex CLI/App, Factory Droid, Gemini CLI, OpenCode, Cursor, GitHub Copilot CLI. Claude Code is the primary/best-supported target.

**Install:** Claude Code (official marketplace): /plugin install superpowers@claude-plugins-official

Claude Code (author's marketplace, gets updates fastest): /plugin marketplace add obra/superpowers-marketplace  then  /plugin install superpowers@superpowers-marketplace

Other harnesses (from README):
- Factory Droid: droid plugin marketplace add https://github.com/obra/superpowers  then  droid plugin install superpowers@superpowers
- Gemini CLI: gemini extensions install https://github.com/obra/superpowers
- GitHub Copilot CLI: copilot plugin marketplace add obra/superpowers-marketplace  then install superpowers
- Cursor: /add-plugin superpowers (in Agent chat)
- Codex CLI: use /plugins and search for Superpowers; Codex App: Plugins sidebar -> + next to Superpowers
- OpenCode: follow .opencode / docs/README.opencode.md in the repo

Note: exact non-Claude commands were read from the README via a summarizer; confirm against the live README before relying on them. No npm/npx package install path exists - it is plugin/extension based, not an npm dependency.

**Maintenance:** Actively maintained; no abandonment/deprecation/rug-pull signals found. Latest release v5.1.0 published 2026-05-04; steady v5.x cadence through Mar-May 2026 (v5.0.0 -> 5.0.7 -> 5.1.0). Repo not archived, not disabled. CORRECTION ON ACTIVITY: GitHub API pushed_at is 2026-06-09 (today), but the most recent commit ON MAIN is dated 2026-05-29 ("Require contributors to disclose authoring environment and target dev branch rather than main") - the 2026-06-09 push reflects a branch/tag/ref push, not a new main commit. So 'last push today' is technically true but overstates main-branch activity; realistic cadence is ~2-3 commits to main in the trailing 30 days (release cadence is bursty, not daily). GOVERNANCE: effectively a solo/BDFL project under Jesse Vincent (GitHub 'obra', Prime Radiant). Contributor distribution heavily concentrated (verified): obra 355 commits, next arittr 24, then clkao 8, 3, 2, 2... Community PRs/issues are flowing (e.g. PR #727, issues #756/#833/#842) but the project is one-maintainer-dependent. Not under an Anthropic/foundation org (though it IS listed in Anthropic's official marketplace). Created 2025-10-09 - a young project (~8 months old). License MIT. Companion repos by the same author: superpowers-marketplace, superpowers-skills [community-editable], superpowers-lab [experimental], superpowers-chrome, superpowers-developing-for-claude-code.

**Popularity:** Extremely high and verified via the GitHub API on 2026-06-09: 222,259 stars, 19,770 forks, 270 open issues, 847 watchers. Created 2025-10-09. Independent coverage corroborates a steep trajectory (reported ~107k stars by Mar 2026, ~150k by Apr 2026, ~170k in a recent aggregator, plus #2 GitHub trending claims), so the ~222k figure is consistent with the curve. This makes it one of the most-starred agentic-coding projects in existence. CAVEAT (stand by skepticism): a star count this high for an ~8-month-old solo plugin is genuinely unusual; the figure is what GitHub returns today, but treat raw stars as a popularity/hype signal, not a proxy for production-grade stability.

**Best for:** Senior/experienced developers and teams shipping non-trivial features (roughly 3+ files or anything needing architectural decisions) where upfront design, comprehensive test coverage, and avoiding context loss matter more than speed. Also strong for people who want a reusable, auditable, cross-tool agent methodology they can fork and tailor. Poor fit for quick one-line fixes, throwaway scripts, rapid prototyping, tight token budgets, or users who want the agent to just-do-the-thing without a planning gate.

**Risks:** SECURITY/TRUST: It is prompt content plus shell-script hooks that run on SessionStart with your shell privileges. The CURRENT hook (hooks/session-start) was independently confirmed benign - it reads a skill file and emits JSON, with no network calls - but review it before/after updates, since a future release or compromised marketplace could ship malicious hook code. A third-party AI-agent-security scanner (Mondoo) rates the 'requesting-code-review' skill MEDIUM risk (40/100): not malware, but flagged because it (a) builds a subagent prompt from an external template file (supply-chain risk if that template is tampered) and (b) delegates control to a spawned code-reviewer subagent - structural concerns inherent to the skill model, not embedded malicious code. The broader Claude Code skills ecosystem has documented supply-chain/credential-theft research (e.g. Snyk's ToxicSkills found ~37% of scanned skills had at least one flaw), so the SKILL.md 'code-blended-with-instructions' format is a recognized attack surface in general. The optional private-journal-mcp is a separate MCP server with broader capabilities; vet it independently before installing. SOLO-MAINTAINER RISK: one-person governance under 'obra' means real abandonment/rug-pull/burnout exposure and slow support if the maintainer steps away; mitigated somewhat by MIT license + public forks but you'd own any fork. SUPPLY-CHAIN: installs from a GitHub-hosted plugin marketplace; pin/inspect versions and avoid blindly auto-updating. LOCK-IN: low at the artifact level (skills are portable markdown, MIT) but workflow lock-in is real - teams adopt its conventions/file layouts and the agent becomes dependent on the injected skills. COMPLEXITY/COST: the disciplined workflow raises token spend and latency and adds cognitive overhead; the forcing-prompt style can make the agent stubborn. MATURITY: ~8-month-old project; very high stars are a hype signal, not proof of production hardening.

**Strengths:**
  - Solves context exhaustion on large features: subagent-per-task + plans persisted to markdown on disk means a 15+ file feature does not blow the main context window (the maintainer's stated original motivation, echoed by independent reviewers).
  - Enforces genuine discipline: hard-gated brainstorming before code, mandatory RED-GREEN-REFACTOR TDD, and inter-task code review catch regressions and surface better designs ('clarifying questions led me to a better approach than my initial instinct').
  - Composable, transparent, hackable: skills are plain markdown SKILL.md files you can read, fork, and extend; there's even a writing-skills skill and a community-editable skills repo (plus a community zh-CN translation, issue #842).
  - Low-magic integration: it is prompts + one SessionStart hook, no compiled binary or background daemon in core - the hook was independently confirmed to do only local file reads with no network calls, making it easy to audit and uninstall.
  - Genuinely multi-harness: same skill library targets Claude Code, Codex, Cursor, Gemini CLI, OpenCode, Copilot CLI, Factory Droid, reducing per-tool methodology drift.
  - Vendor-recognized: accepted into Anthropic's official Claude Code plugin marketplace (PR #148), which is a credibility signal beyond raw stars.
  - MIT licensed and free; strong momentum and a large community (Discord, issues), so techniques get refined fast.

**Weaknesses:**
  - Heavy process overhead: independent reviewers and the docs themselves call it 'overkill' for single-file changes, quick bug fixes, and small refactors - the brainstorm/plan/execute cycle adds latency and token cost without proportional benefit on routine work.
  - Token/time cost: multi-phase planning + spawning fresh subagents + mandatory tests/reviews consume substantially more tokens and wall-clock time than ad-hoc prompting (a real cost on metered/API usage).
  - Coercive prompt design: skills use aggressive forcing language ('even a 1% chance a skill might apply, you ABSOLUTELY MUST invoke the skill', 'not negotiable'). This can feel rigid, can over-trigger the workflow on trivial asks, and relies on prompt-pressure that models may still ignore or that may conflict with the user's intent.
  - Behavior depends on a SessionStart hook injecting context every session - if hooks fail, are disabled, or the harness handles additionalContext differently, the methodology degrades silently.
  - Deprecation churn from the fast pace: legacy slash commands were removed in v5.1.0, but post-release issues (#756, #833) report installs still surfacing deprecated command aliases that confuse users or block skill invocation - migration friction is real.
  - Best-supported only on Claude Code; non-Claude harness support is real but more likely to have edge cases (the hook already carries per-platform workarounds, e.g. a documented bash 5.3+ heredoc hang).
  - Single-maintainer bus factor: 355 of the commits are one person's; long-term continuity, support latency, and roadmap all hinge on one individual.
  - Methodology, not a guarantee: it shapes the agent's process but cannot ensure correct code; the rigidity can also impede genuinely exploratory or throwaway prototyping.

**Fact-check confidence:** high

**Verification notes:** RE-VERIFIED the riskiest claims on 2026-06-09 via live GitHub API + raw repo files + web search. CONFIRMED: (1) POPULARITY - GitHub API returns stars 222,259 / forks 19,770 / open_issues 270 / watchers 847; created 2025-10-09; archived=false, disabled=false; license MIT. Original profile's ~222,255/~19,769 was within rounding of today's exact figures; updated to exact. Independent reports (107k Mar, 150k Apr, 170k recently) corroborate the trajectory, so the figure is plausible despite being unusually large for an 8-month solo plugin (flagged as hype signal, not stability proof). (2) MAINTENANCE - latest release v5.1.0 confirmed 2026-05-04 (releases API). NOT abandoned: web search found active PRs/issues (PR #727, issues #756/#833/#842) and ongoing refactors; no rug-pull/deprecation-of-whole-project signals. CORRECTION: API pushed_at=2026-06-09 but the most recent commit on MAIN is 2026-05-29 - the 'today' push is a ref/branch push, not a new main commit; adjusted maintenance text to avoid overstating activity. Contributors API confirmed solo dominance: obra 355, arittr 24, clkao 8 (rest <=3). (3) INTEGRATION - plugin.json confirmed v5.1.0/MIT/jesse@fsck.com; CORRECTED its description to the actual text 'Core skills library for Claude Code: TDD, debugging, collaboration patterns, and proven techniques'. v5.1.0 release notes explicitly confirm 'Legacy slash commands removed' (supports the no-slash-commands claim). hooks.json confirmed (SessionStart, matcher startup|clear|compact, synchronous, run-hook.cmd session-start). session-start script re-read: injects using-superpowers as additionalContext, per-harness JSON (Claude Code nested, Cursor snake_case, Copilot top-level), legacy-dir warning, and crucially NO network calls (benign). Official-marketplace acceptance confirmed via anthropics/claude-plugins-official PR #148 (reported 2026-01-15). Skill count confirmed = 14 folders on main. (4) SECURITY - NO malware reports found. NEW concrete third-party signal added: Mondoo AI-agent-security rates 'requesting-code-review' MEDIUM (40/100) for external-template supply-chain + subagent-delegation concerns (explicitly NOT malicious code). Broader-ecosystem research (Snyk ToxicSkills ~37% flawed) added as context for the SKILL.md attack-surface point. COULD NOT independently re-verify the exact non-Claude harness install command syntax byte-for-byte (still summarizer-sourced) - left flagged. Overall the original profile was highly accurate; changes are exact-number refresh, plugin.json description fix, main-commit-vs-pushed_at clarification, and addition of the citable Mondoo/Snyk security findings.

### GitHub Spec Kit
`https://github.com/github/spec-kit`

**Category:** Spec-Driven Development (SDD) toolkit / methodology scaffolding for AI coding agents — multi-agent, not Claude-Code-specific

**One-liner:** An official GitHub open-source CLI (specify) that scaffolds a structured "spec before code" workflow — Constitution → Specify → Plan → Tasks → Implement — into your repo as slash commands and Markdown artifacts, for 30+ AI coding agents including Claude Code.

**Philosophy:** Spec-Driven Development inverts the usual flow: the specification is the primary, "living, executable" artifact and code is a downstream product of it, rather than specs being throwaway documentation. The methodology originated in John Lam's research at GitHub/Microsoft on making LLM-driven development more deterministic, and was launched as open source by Den Delimarsky and John Lam in late August / early September 2025 (GitHub blog, Sept 2 2025). The stated premise is that coding agents are "exceptional at pattern completion, but not at mind reading" and behave like "literal-minded pair programmers," so vague prompts like "add photo sharing to my app" force the model to guess at unstated intent. Spec Kit counters this with multi-step refinement and organizational guardrails (a project "constitution") instead of one-shot prompting, aiming to replace "vibe coding" with reviewable, intent-anchored generation. Explicit design goal: technology/agent independence and "no lock-in." (Correction vs prior profile: credit John Lam as research originator/co-creator alongside Delimarsky, not Delimarsky alone.)

**How it works:** Workflow is a linked chain where each phase emits a Markdown artifact that feeds the next. Slash commands (all prefixed /speckit. in current versions): /speckit.constitution (principles → .specify/memory/constitution.md), /speckit.specify (requirements → spec.md), /speckit.clarify (optional), /speckit.plan (architecture → plan.md + research.md, data-model.md, contracts/, quickstart.md), /speckit.tasks (→ tasks.md), /speckit.analyze (consistency check), /speckit.checklist (quality gates), /speckit.implement (generates code task-by-task), /speckit.taskstoissues (→ GitHub issues). All nine command names verified against the current README. Artifacts: project-level .specify/ tree (memory/, scripts/bash + PowerShell, templates/, extensions/, presets/) plus per-feature specs/[NNN-feature-name]/ folders. No built-in subagent orchestration in core; only a community 'MAQA' preset advertises multi-agent + QA gates. Customization precedence (template resolution priority stack): project overrides > presets > extensions > core templates. Presets/extensions are added via `specify preset add` / `specify extension add` and stack with priority ordering.

**Claude Code integration:** Multi-tool, NOT Claude-Code-only. Claude Code is one of 30+ supported integrations (Copilot, Gemini, Codex, Cursor, Qwen, opencode, Kiro, Goose, Mistral/Vibe, Forge, Tabnine, Pi, Qoder, Windsurf, etc.). Integration mechanism (verified against README): running `specify init` with the Claude integration writes the /speckit.* commands as plain Markdown prompt files into Claude Code's project command directory (.claude/commands/), the standard Claude Code custom-slash-command format — so the commands appear natively as /speckit.specify etc. in the Claude Code TUI. Spec Kit also supports a "skills mode" via --integration-options="--skills", which installs agent skills (e.g. .claude/skills/<name>/SKILL.md) instead of command files for agents that support skills; in current Claude Code, commands and skills both produce an invocable /command. It is NOT a Claude Code native plugin, NOT an MCP server, and uses NO hooks — integration is purely prompt/Markdown files dropped into agent directories by the Python CLI (the README describes it as a "toolkit," not a plugin/MCP/hook). Known gap: `specify init` does not auto-generate CLAUDE.md, so Claude lacks built-in awareness of Spec Kit vocabulary/artifact locations unless you add it manually.

**Install:** Prereqs (verified): Python 3.11+, Git, and uv (recommended) or pipx, plus a supported agent (Linux/macOS/Windows). Persistent install: `uv tool install specify-cli --from git+https://github.com/github/spec-kit.git@v0.10.0` (pin a tag). One-shot via uvx: `uvx --from git+https://github.com/github/spec-kit.git specify init my-project --integration claude`. Other init forms: `specify init . --integration claude --integration-options="--skills"` (skills mode), `specify init --here --force --integration claude` (current dir). Self-management: `specify self check`, `specify self upgrade` (also `--dry-run` and `--tag vX.Y.Z`). IMPORTANT breaking change in v0.10.0 (verified on the releases page / changelog): the legacy `--ai` flag (plus `--ai-commands-dir`, `--ai-skills`) seen in older blogs/tutorials was REMOVED; use `--integration claude` and `--integration-options` now. Also as of v0.10.0 the git extension is opt-in: run `specify extension add git` if you want it (the old `--no-git` flag was removed; `specify init` no longer auto-installs the git extension). Discover agents/extensions with `specify integration list` / `specify extension search` / `specify preset search`.

**Maintenance:** Very actively maintained. Owned by the `github` organization (org-governed, not a solo project), MIT licensed, not archived, not disabled. Created Aug 21 2025; ~160 releases. Latest release v0.10.0 published 2026-06-09 (the current date); v0.9.0 through v0.9.5 all shipped within the preceding ~1.5 weeks (June 1–5) and v0.8.18 on May 29 — extremely high release cadence. Codebase is predominantly Python (the specify CLI) plus shell/PowerShell scripts. CORRECTION vs prior profile: the original creators were Den Delimarsky (PM/evangelist) AND John Lam (research originator); Delimarsky is now Member of Technical Staff at Anthropic (leading Model Context Protocol and Claude SDKs) — BUT he remains the single dominant active maintainer of spec-kit. His work account `@localden` is the #1 contributor by a wide margin (~361 commits per the contributors API), followed by `mnriem` (Manfred Riem, ~183). So a de facto lead maintainer IS identifiable; abandonment risk is therefore even lower than 'org-governed' framing implies, but bus-factor is concentrated in roughly two people rather than being broadly distributed across the GitHub org. There is still no explicit public GOVERNANCE/CODEOWNERS doc naming successors. ~398 open issues and ~150 open PRs at research time indicate heavy churn.

**Popularity:** Approximately 111,000 GitHub stars (live repo page ~111k; GitHub API reported 110,781 at API-snapshot time) and ~9,800 forks, ~586 watchers. Rapid growth trajectory corroborated by third-party trackers (~71k Feb 2026, ~90k+ May 2026, ~111k June 2026; star-history showed a stale ~92.4k cache). One of the most-starred AI-coding-workflow repos and reportedly among the top-starred repositories in the entire `github` org — strong GitHub-brand-driven adoption since Aug 2025.

**Best for:** Greenfield projects or self-contained net-new features, especially for solo developers, small teams, or product-owner/non-engineer roles who want enforced structure, an auditable spec→plan→tasks trail, and reviewable incremental changes. Also good for teams standardizing an AI workflow across multiple agents/IDEs and wanting to avoid agent lock-in. Poor fit for quick fixes, rapid iterative/exploratory work, or large brownfield enterprise codebases with heavy existing architecture and cross-team review/compliance constraints.

**Risks:** ["Security/trust: install is via `uv tool install`/`uvx` from a git source running Python plus shell/PowerShell scripts that write command files into your repo (.specify/, .claude/) and can convert tasks to GitHub issues — review what it scaffolds; pin a version tag (@v0.10.0) rather than tracking HEAD to avoid surprise breaking changes. NOTE: no spec-kit/specify-cli-specific CVE, security advisory, malware, or supply-chain rug-pull was found as of June 2026; this is a general caution about executing third-party install scripts, not a reported incident. (Unrelated 2026 CVEs exist for GitHub itself, Gemini CLI, and a Claude Code credential-disclosure issue, but none affect spec-kit.)", "Maintainer/bus-factor: low-but-non-zero — original creator Den Delimarsky moved to Anthropic yet remains the dominant active contributor via @localden; effective maintenance is concentrated in ~2 people (localden, mnriem) under the GitHub org with no published successor/governance doc. Abandonment risk is low while Delimarsky stays engaged.", "Lock-in: low by design (tool-agnostic, MIT, plain Markdown artifacts you own), but methodological lock-in is real — adopting the .specify/ structure and SDD process is a workflow commitment, and migrating accumulated specs out is non-trivial", "Complexity/process overhead: the multi-phase ceremony can cost more than it saves on small or fast-changing work and can mislead with a false sense of rigor while still emitting buggy code (Scott Logic)", "Volatility: weekly releases with breaking flag/behavior changes (e.g. --ai removed, git opt-in in v0.10.0) mean documentation drift and upgrade friction; treat third-party tutorials (especially those using the old --ai flag) as potentially outdated"]

**Strengths:**
  - Backed by GitHub org with MIT license and a very high release cadence (v0.9.x weekly, v0.10.0 on the current date) — low abandonment risk and quick fixes
  - Still actively driven by its original creator: Delimarsky's @localden account is the top contributor by far even after his move to Anthropic, so the project has a clearly engaged lead rather than being abandoned to the org
  - Genuinely multi-agent / tool-agnostic: 30+ integrations (Claude Code, Copilot, Gemini, Codex, Cursor, opencode, Qwen, Kiro, Goose, Mistral, etc.) with explicit 'no lock-in' design — you can switch agents
  - Integrates with Claude Code through the native, standard .claude/commands/ Markdown slash-command format (and optional skills mode) — nothing proprietary, easy to inspect/modify
  - Forces explicit intent: produces reviewable, focused artifacts (spec/plan/tasks) instead of opaque thousand-line code dumps; surfaces hidden assumptions (e.g. data-migration needs) early
  - Strong scaffolding for greenfield features and for non-engineers/product owners who want structure
  - Extensible via presets/extensions with a clear override precedence; can convert tasks to GitHub issues (/speckit.taskstoissues)
  - No known security advisory, CVE, malware, or supply-chain incident affecting spec-kit / specify-cli as of June 2026

**Weaknesses:**
  - Heavy overhead: a hands-on Scott Logic review (Colin Eberhardt, Nov 2025) measured ~33m30 of agent time, 2,577 lines of markdown and 3.5 hrs of review for the first increment vs ~8 min agent time and a ~15-min review for iterative prompting, concluding 'I am a lot more productive without SDD, around ten times faster'
  - Waterfall-like rigidity: critics call it sequential up-front design that fights agile iteration ('reinvented waterfall')
  - Brittle to change: reviewers report a single upstream requirement pivot can invalidate the specification, with downstream tasks inheriting broken assumptions
  - Optimized for net-new/greenfield; weak for brownfield — it doesn't account for established architecture or years of accumulated business rules; thin evidence for large-scale enterprise/brownfield adoption (Martinelli enterprise critique)
  - Specs do not guarantee quality: even with extensive specs, the Scott Logic implementation still shipped 'a small, and very obvious, bug' — the 'detailed specs prevent defects' premise didn't fully hold
  - Fast-moving and immature (still pre-1.0): ~160 releases in <1 year with breaking flag changes (--ai removed, git now opt-in, --no-git removed in v0.10.0) means tutorials/blogs go stale fast and upgrades can break workflows; ~398 open issues / ~150 open PRs
  - Not deeply integrated with Claude Code (no plugin/MCP/hooks/subagents); CLAUDE.md is not auto-generated, so Claude isn't automatically aware of Spec Kit conventions
  - Maintainer concentration: despite GitHub-org ownership, real activity is concentrated in ~2 contributors (localden, mnriem), so practical bus-factor is modest

**Fact-check confidence:** high

**Verification notes:** Re-verified all riskiest claims on 2026-06-09 against live web sources (unauthenticated GitHub API curl was rate-limited; verified via WebFetch of github.com pages, raw README, releases page, contributors API, and the GitHub blog/docs). CONFIRMED: MIT, not archived/disabled, created 2025-08-21, default branch main; latest release v0.10.0 published 2026-06-09 with v0.9.0–v0.9.5 (June 1–5) and v0.8.18 (May 29) preceding — high cadence confirmed; v0.10.0 breaking changes confirmed (legacy --ai/--ai-commands-dir/--ai-skills removed in favor of --integration/--integration-options; git extension now opt-in via `specify extension add git`; --no-git removed); 30+ agent integrations; all nine /speckit.* command names from the README; install/prereq commands (Python 3.11+, uv/uvx/pipx, Git); Claude integration writes Markdown slash-command files into .claude/commands/ with optional --integration-options=\"--skills\" skills mode; NOT a plugin/MCP/hooks; Scott Logic metrics (33m30 agent time, 2,577 lines markdown, 3.5 hr review vs ~8 min / ~15 min iterative; 'around ten times faster'; an 'obvious bug' shipped despite specs). POPULARITY: profile's 110,781 stars matches the API snapshot; live repo page now shows ~111,000 (slight upward drift) — trajectory corroborated by third-party trackers (~71k Feb, ~90k+ May, ~111k June; star-history cache showed a stale ~92.4k). CORRECTIONS/REFINEMENTS: (1) Maintenance/governance — the prior profile understated continuity: Delimarsky (now Anthropic Member of Technical Staff on MCP/Claude SDKs) is STILL the dominant maintainer; his work account @localden is the #1 spec-kit contributor (~361 commits), with mnriem (~183) second — so a de facto lead IS identifiable and abandonment risk is lower than 'org only' framing, though practical bus-factor is ~2 people. (2) Origin — corrected to credit John Lam as research originator/co-creator alongside Delimarsky (project 'born out of John Lam's research'; co-launched). (3) Security — affirmatively stated that NO spec-kit-specific CVE/advisory/malware/supply-chain incident was found; unrelated 2026 GitHub/Gemini-CLI/Claude-Code CVEs surfaced in searches but do not touch spec-kit. COULD NOT CONFIRM: a published successor/governance/CODEOWNERS doc; exact present-day star figure drifts slightly above 110,781.

**Sources:** https://github.com/github/spec-kit · https://api.github.com/repos/github/spec-kit · https://api.github.com/repos/github/spec-kit/contributors · https://github.com/github/spec-kit/releases · https://raw.githubusercontent.com/github/spec-kit/main/README.md · https://github.github.com/spec-kit/ · https://github.blog/ai-and-ml/generative-ai/spec-driven-development-with-ai-get-started-with-a-new-open-source-toolkit/ · https://developer.microsoft.com/blog/spec-driven-development-spec-kit · https://den.dev/blog/github-spec-kit/ · https://github.com/dend · https://github.com/jflam · https://blog.scottlogic.com/2025/11/26/putting-spec-kit-through-its-paces-radical-idea-or-reinvented-waterfall.html

### wshobson/agents
`https://github.com/wshobson/agents`

**Category:** Agentic-coding framework / plugin marketplace — a curated library of subagents, skills, slash commands, and multi-agent orchestration workflows for Claude Code (and 5 other AI coding harnesses). Not an executable runtime: it is a content/configuration library (Markdown + JSON) interpreted natively by each harness.

**One-liner:** A large, opinionated marketplace of production-ready Claude Code plugins (subagents + skills + slash commands + orchestrators) authored once in Markdown and shipped natively to Claude Code, Codex CLI, Cursor, OpenCode, Gemini CLI, and GitHub Copilot.

**Philosophy:** "One source of truth, many harnesses": author agentic content once in Markdown under plugins/ and generate idiomatic, harness-native artifacts (not lowest-common-denominator translations) for each of 6 AI coding tools. Within that, favor granular single-purpose plugins optimized for minimal token usage (selective loading) over a monolithic dump, and assign each agent the cheapest model that can do its job (task-complexity-driven model tiering). Composition/orchestration emerges from many small specialist agents coordinated by workflow commands rather than one giant generalist prompt.

**How it works:** It is fundamentally a content/configuration library, not an executable framework — there is no runtime binary; everything is Markdown + JSON config that each harness interprets natively.

COMPONENTS (current README + commit log, June 2026, CONFIRMED): 84 plugins (82 local + 2 external via git-subdir), 192 agents, 156 skills, 102 commands, 16 multi-agent orchestrator workflows. A June-2-2026 commit explicitly synced docs to "84/192/156". Counts have changed repeatedly as the repo evolved; earlier 2025 snapshots cite e.g. 62-63 plugins / 76-112 agents / 44-47 skills / 15 workflows — treat exact counts as version-dependent.

SUBAGENTS: Each agent is a Markdown file with YAML front-matter: `name`, `description` (drives auto-delegation), optional `model` (haiku/sonnet/opus), optional `tools` allowlist. Claude Code reads the description and auto-delegates matching tasks to the subagent in its own isolated context window; users can also invoke explicitly. Agents span architecture, languages, infra, security, data, ML, business, SEO, blockchain, game dev, quant trading, etc.

MODEL-TIER STRATEGY: Agents are pre-assigned to models by task complexity. README (June 2026) cites a four-tier scheme: Tier 1 'Opus 4.7' (high-stakes work), a user-chosen tier, Sonnet (general dev/test/docs), and Haiku (cheap operational tasks). Specific model IDs track whatever was current at authoring time and go stale.

SLASH COMMANDS: 102 commands for scaffolding, security scans, test generation, setup, etc., installed per-plugin.

ORCHESTRATORS / 'PHASES': 16 multi-agent workflow commands that coordinate several subagents via sequential/parallel/conditional/review patterns. No rigid global phase pipeline; orchestration is per-workflow.

ARTIFACTS IT CREATES: For Claude Code it installs plugin folders (`.claude-plugin/plugin.json`, `agents/`, `commands/`, `skills/`) and a `marketplace.json`. A single Markdown 'source of truth' under `plugins/` is compiled (Makefile build, repo language reported ~90% Python) into harness-native outputs: `.codex-plugin/`, `.cursor-plugin/` + `.cursor/rules/`, `.opencode/`, Gemini TOML, and Copilot artifacts (Copilot maps to the GPT-5 model family per the README harness table). A `plugin-eval` framework (three-layer: static / LLM-judge / Monte-Carlo) is included for quality-checking plugins.

**Claude Code integration:** Claude Code is the explicit source-of-truth / first-class harness — but the project is NOT Claude-Code-only. It is multi-tool: the same Markdown is shipped natively to OpenAI Codex CLI, Cursor, OpenCode, Gemini CLI, and GitHub Copilot (6 harnesses total; the GitHub repo's one-line description lists only the first five, but the README badges/harness table explicitly include Copilot).

Integration with Claude Code uses NATIVE Claude Code plugin primitives — not a wrapper CLI, not npx, and not an MCP server of its own:
- Plugin marketplace: installed via Claude Code's built-in `/plugin marketplace add` + `/plugin install`.
- Subagents: native Claude Code subagent files (YAML front-matter) that Claude auto-delegates to.
- Skills: native Claude Code Agent Skills (modular, progressive-disclosure SKILL.md packages).
- Slash commands: native Claude Code custom slash commands.
- MCP: it references external-memory / cross-tool integrations (e.g. a 'Pensyve'/git-subdir external plugin), but the agents project does not itself run as an MCP server — MCP is optional/peripheral.
- Hooks: CONFIRMED no Claude Code hooks ship with the project. The docs/ directory (agent-skills.md, agents.md, architecture.md, authoring.md, harnesses.md, plugin-eval.md, plugins.md, round-trip-results.md, usage.md) contains no hooks file or hooks directory, and the README does not mention hooks.

LEGACY method still works: cloning the repo into `~/.claude/agents/` (flat files) so all subagents load globally — but this is what caused the context-bloat problem (see weaknesses), and the marketplace/per-plugin model now exists specifically to avoid loading everything at once. Note: even the per-plugin model has reproduced the bloat when many plugins are installed (issues #80, #93).

**Install:** Claude Code (recommended, selective):
  /plugin marketplace add wshobson/agents
  /plugin install python-development   (or any specific plugin, e.g. full-stack-development, security-scanning)

Claude Code (legacy, loads ALL agents globally — NOT recommended, causes context bloat):
  cd ~/.claude && git clone https://github.com/wshobson/agents.git

Other harnesses (clone + Makefile build; commands per README — re-verify against current docs/harnesses.md before use):
  gh repo clone wshobson/agents ~/agents && cd ~/agents && make install-opencode   # OpenCode
  make generate HARNESS=gemini && gemini extensions install .                        # Gemini CLI
  Codex CLI / Cursor / Copilot: generated artifact directories (.codex-plugin/, .cursor-plugin/, Copilot artifacts) consumed natively.

Companion repo: wshobson/commands (standalone slash commands for Claude Code).

WARNING: There is no tagged release. To pin a known-good state you must use a git commit SHA (see maintenance).

**Maintenance:** Actively maintained and NOT abandoned. Live GitHub API (fetched 2026-06-09, authoritative): created 2025-07-24, pushed_at 2026-06-08, default branch main, archived=false, fork=false, open issues only 9. The most recent commit on main is 2026-06-05 (a Dependabot Starlette bump merge); a June-2 commit synced component counts to 84/192/156. So development is current and ongoing.

Dependency hygiene: the repo runs Dependabot and GitHub Actions CI on its Python `plugin-eval` tooling — an active-maintenance signal (security patches are being merged).

Governance: solo maintainer / personal project — owner is Seth Hobson (GitHub @wshobson, homepage sethhobson.com, Senior AI Engineer at United Data Technologies / UDT, ex-Apple). Single-author repo, not an org or foundation. Key risk: bus-factor of 1.

Releases/versioning: CONFIRMED no GitHub Releases and no git tags (both API endpoints returned empty arrays on 2026-06-09). The project is continuously updated on `main` with no versioned releases or advertised cadence. To pin a known-good state you must reference a commit SHA.

Fork history: the repo itself is an original (fork=false) but has spawned many downstream forks/mirrors (e.g. mvandermeulen/wshobson-agents, aiwonglab/claude_code_agents, destenson/wshobson--agents, chusri/claude-code-agents) — third-party copies, not the canonical source.

Note: the project has gone through major structural churn — it began (mid-2025) as a flat collection of subagent Markdown files and was re-architected into the multi-harness plugin marketplace. Component counts have changed many times, so docs/blogs citing specific numbers go stale fast.

**Popularity:** Approximately 36,500+ GitHub stars. Live API 2026-06-09 (authoritative): 36,555 stars, 3,955 forks, 312 watchers (subscribers), 9 open issues. One of the most-starred Claude Code agent/plugin repos. Growth trajectory: 17,647 stars (ecosyste.ms snapshot synced 2025-10-13) to 36,555 (June 2026) — roughly doubled in ~8 months. Some third-party pages quote stale figures (14.5k in one of the maintainer's own profile blurbs, 17.6k, 19.9k, 34.3k, 35.3k); the live API number is authoritative.

**Best for:** Developers and teams already using Claude Code (or one of the supported harnesses) who want a broad, ready-made library of specialized subagents/skills/commands and don't want to author their own from scratch — especially polyglot/full-stack engineers who touch many domains. Best used selectively: install only the 1-3 plugins relevant to the current project. Good for experienced users who will explicitly invoke agents and curate which load. Less suitable for beginners who install everything (context blows up per issue #93) or for anyone needing a deterministic, versioned, governance-backed dependency.

**Risks:** ["Trust / bus-factor: single solo maintainer (Seth Hobson, Senior AI Engineer at UDT). No org/foundation governance. If he stops, the repo stalls; you'd rely on community forks. NO evidence of malicious/rug-pull/abandonment behavior found, but supply-chain trust rests on one person.", "Supply-chain / security: agents are prompts with tool-access front-matter and can carry tool allowlists; subagents/skills can instruct Claude to run shell commands, read files, or call MCP/network. Installing 100+ third-party prompts means trusting all of them. This is inherent to any prompt/skill marketplace, not specific to this repo. Note the broader ecosystem context: Check Point disclosed (Feb 2026) that a malicious repo could inject Claude Code Hook/.mcp.json config to run shell commands before trust dialogs, and there are active fake-'Claude Code'-installer malware campaigns (Bitdefender/Trend Micro) — none of these are about wshobson/agents, but they underscore why you should review specific plugins, pin a commit SHA, and prefer minimal selective installs. NO known malicious content in this repo.", "Context/performance risk (issue #93, Closed): installing broadly can render Claude Code unusable (~404.1k vs 15.0k tokens) and silently degrade output quality as the window fills.", "Complexity: 84 plugins / 192 agents / 16 orchestrators is a lot of surface area; non-determinism of which agent fires makes behavior hard to predict and debug.", "Lock-in: LOW relative to single-tool frameworks (it's MIT Markdown shipped to 6 harnesses), but you take a dependency on this curation style and on Claude Code's plugin/subagent feature set. Mitigate by copying only the agents you actually use into your own repo.", "Versioning risk: CONFIRMED no releases and no tags, so you track a moving main branch unless you pin a SHA."]

**Strengths:**
  - Huge breadth: 192 domain-expert subagents + 156 skills + 102 slash commands covering virtually every dev domain (backend, frontend, infra, security, ML, data, blockchain, game dev, quant, business/SEO) — strong out-of-the-box coverage.
  - Uses NATIVE Claude Code primitives (marketplace plugins, subagents, skills, slash commands) — no proprietary runtime, low magic, easy to inspect (it's just Markdown + JSON).
  - Per-plugin selective install is the intended way to avoid the original context-bloat problem: you load only the agents/skills you need rather than all of them.
  - Multi-harness from one source of truth: same content works in Codex CLI, Cursor, OpenCode, Gemini CLI, and GitHub Copilot — reduces lock-in to a single tool.
  - Cost-aware model-tier assignment (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku by task) is thoughtful and can save money.
  - Pre-built multi-agent orchestrator workflows (16 of them: full-stack, incident response, security, etc.) give ready-made coordination patterns.
  - Includes a three-layer plugin-eval quality framework (static + LLM-judge + Monte-Carlo) — unusually rigorous for a community repo.
  - MIT-licensed; very actively maintained (commits within days, Dependabot security bumps merged, only 9 open issues); large community/fork ecosystem (~3,955 forks).

**Weaknesses:**
  - DOCUMENTED context-window exhaustion (issue #93, now Closed): installing broadly (legacy clone, or installing several plugins) loads ~404.1k tokens of agent descriptions vs a 15.0k recommended budget (~27x over), making Claude Code unusable ('Context low - Run /compact') until you uninstall. Related issue #80 reports agents being double-installed. You MUST install selectively.
  - Auto-delegation is unreliable in practice: a well-known limitation of Claude Code subagents generally is that the main agent often handles tasks itself and rarely summons subagents unless explicitly named — so much of the value requires manual invocation.
  - Subagents run in isolated contexts and each only knows its own task, with no shared project context — can cause duplicated work, inconsistent decisions, and high token burn.
  - Quality is breadth-over-depth and prompt-only: agents are essentially curated system prompts; effectiveness varies by domain and there's no guarantee any given agent beats stock Claude on your task.
  - High churn: component counts and structure have changed repeatedly (flat files -> marketplace), so tutorials/blogs go stale and your setup can drift.
  - Solo maintainer (bus-factor 1); CONFIRMED no tagged releases/versioning, so pinning a known-good state relies on git SHAs.
  - Token cost can balloon with multi-agent orchestrators and large file reads.

**Fact-check confidence:** high
